Hi. Thank you so much for having me. Um, I hope I'm in the right place for this. 

What brings me in is... well, lately I just feel like I can’t really settle in my own body anymore? It's just really hard to stay put. 

Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay. I just thought it might be good to talk to someone about it, if that's alright.

It's probably just a small thing, honestly, but I've been noticing it a lot when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and handle paperwork, or when I need to sit down to figure out shared household plans. It's just really hard to stay put. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay. I just thought it might be good to talk to someone about it, if that's alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, um... restless is exactly it. Like I was just mentioning, it's this feeling that I really can't settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I'm supposed to just sit quietly to handle paperwork, or... or when I need to sit down for those shared household plans. \n\nIt's like I just have this urge to move? Um, yes... I think so. I feel a little embarrassed admitting it, to be honest, because I really do try my best to pay attention. I promise I'm not just trying to brush things off.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Oh, um... wow, when did it start? I guess... if I'm really looking back, I think it was already a part of me way back in elementary school. 

I feel a little silly admitting this, because I really did try to be a good student, I promise! But... well, I was kind of the little girl with the constantly messy desk. Oh... um... that's a tough one to admit. I really, really care about being respectful, and I know how rude it can seem to cut people off. I always try to be a good listener, I promise.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I guess I sort of already mentioned it, but... yes, it does happen with other people sometimes, not just my husband. 

I really do try so hard to be polite, because I know how it looks when someone just talks over you. But... if I'm in a group, or even just chatting with someone I don't know as well, I might still accidentally jump in or finish their sentence for them. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. 

I always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.

My teachers would sometimes write notes home saying I talked out of turn, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat. \n\nI know all kids are a little squirmy and talkative, so I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it was. I wasn't trying to be disruptive, I just had a lot of energy, I think! But yes, it goes back that far. Is that... is that the kind of timeline you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... that's a tough one to admit. I really, really care about being respectful, and I know how rude it can seem to cut people off. I always try to be a good listener, I promise. \n\nBut... well, sometimes with my husband, or even friends, I'll just... jump into the conversation. Or I'll finish their thoughts for them. It's not because I'm trying to be bossy or anything! It's just... the response just sort of pops out of my mouth before I've even had a chance to tell myself to wait. \n\nI feel terrible every time it happens, because I don't want anyone to think I don't care about what they have to say. I just get so eager to agree or share, you know? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible conversationalist. Is that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers in situations other than with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess I sort of already mentioned it, but... yes, it does happen with other people sometimes, not just my husband. \n\nI really do try so hard to be polite, because I know how it looks when someone just talks over you. But... if I'm in a group, or even just chatting with someone I don't know as well, I might still accidentally jump in or finish their sentence for them. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. \n\nI always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're not talking to someone, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... doing things without thinking them through? I... I don't think so? I really try to be careful and responsible, especially with, you know, daily life stuff. I'm a stay-at-home mom, so I have to keep things running smoothly, and I try my best to be organized. \n\nI mean, I guess everyone has moments where they buy something small at the store without planning to, or maybe decide to bake cookies on a whim, but... no, I wouldn't say I do anything big or risky without thinking it over. I always want to make sure I'm making good choices for my family. \n\nSo, I don't think I really have a problem with that kind of impulsivity. I hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had difficulty keeping a job or maintaining relationships because of your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... well, since I'm a stay-at-home mom, I haven't been in a traditional job for a while, so I don't have to worry about a boss firing me or anything like that. But... oh, this is a little hard to admit, because I really, really pride myself on being helpful and dependable. \n\nI guess... well, the fallout lately has been that my family at home, and the people in my volunteer circles... they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me as much as I'd want them to. \n\nI try so, so hard, I promise I do! I always have the best intentions. But sometimes I just... I keep letting things fall through the cracks. Because of that, I've actually had to step back from some of my volunteer responsibilities recently. I just didn't want to keep accidentally letting people down, you know? \n\nIt feels really awful to say that out loud, because I hate the idea of being seen as flaky or unreliable. I really hope you don't think I'm just lazy or making excuses. I just want to do a good job for everyone and keep the peace. Does... does that make sense for what you were asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'm a stay-at-home mom, so I have to keep things running smoothly, and I try my best to be organized. \n\nI mean, I guess everyone has moments where they buy something small at the store without planning to, or maybe decide to bake cookies on a whim, but... no, I wouldn't say I do anything big or risky without thinking it over. I always want to make sure I'm making good choices for my family. \n\nSo, I don't think I really have a problem with that kind of impulsivity. I hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had difficulty keeping a job or maintaining relationships because of your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... well, since I'm a stay-at-home mom, I haven't been in a traditional job for a while, so I don't have to worry about a boss firing me or anything like that. But... oh, this is a little hard to admit, because I really, really pride myself on being helpful and dependable. \n\nI guess... well, the fallout lately has been that my family at home, and the people in my volunteer circles... they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me as much as I'd want them to. \n\nI try so, so hard, I promise I do! I always have the best intentions. But sometimes I just... I keep letting things fall through the cracks. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I think I've always been a little bit... you know, busy-minded? But it definitely hasn't always been this hard to manage. 

Actually... and I really hope this doesn't sound like I'm complaining or making excuses, because my husband is wonderful and I love our life... but it has been noticeably worse since we got married. That was about a year and five months ago. When our routines merged, it just... it became a lot harder to keep everything organized. 

I really, really wanted to make the transition seamless for everyone and be a great wife, but combining our daily lives just made things feel a bit more overwhelming for me, and that scattered feeling definitely spiked. 

Is it normal for things to get a little harder when you have to adjust to someone else's schedule like that? I just don't want you to think I'm not trying my absolute best to adapt. Does... does that help explain the timeline a little bit?

Oh... um, distress? I mean... I wouldn't say it's like, severe emotional pain or anything like that. I really don't want to sound dramatic.

I feel awful when it happens because I don't want people to think I'm ignoring them. Did I... did I answer your question okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're having trouble focusing or sitting still?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, anxious? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I definitely feel bad about it, like I mentioned, because I don't want to let anyone down or seem lazy. But I wouldn't say I feel worried or, you know, panicked about it. \n\nIt's more just... frustrating, I guess? But I try to stay positive! I really don't think it's a big anxiety thing, if that's what you mean. I'm generally a pretty happy person, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not being too confusing.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble sitting still and focusing?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, when did it start? I guess... if I'm really looking back, I think it was already a part of me way back in elementary school. \n\nI feel a little silly admitting this, because I really did try to be a good student, I promise! But... well, I was kind of the little girl with the constantly messy desk. And I was always forgetting my permission slips, things like that. My teachers would sometimes write notes home saying I talked out of turn, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat. \n\nI know all kids are a little squirmy and talkative, so I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it was. It's just... the response just sort of pops out of my mouth before I've even had a chance to tell myself to wait. \n\nI feel terrible every time it happens, because I don't want anyone to think I don't care about what they have to say. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. \n\nI always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... But... if I'm in a group, or even just chatting with someone I don't know as well, I might still accidentally jump in or finish their sentence for them. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. \n\nI always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're not talking to someone, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... doing things without thinking them through? I... I don't think so? I really try to be careful and responsible, especially with, you know, daily life stuff. Does... does that help explain the timeline a little bit?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are causing you a lot of distress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, distress? I mean... I wouldn't say it's like, severe emotional pain or anything like that. I really don't want to sound dramatic. \n\nI guess it's just... well, it's frustrating, you know? Because I really want to be on top of everything for my husband and my kids. When I mess up or forget something important, I do feel pretty bad about it. It makes me feel like I'm letting them down, and that part is hard. But I wouldn't say I'm in deep distress or anything. I just want to figure out how to do better for them. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making too big a deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing or your restlessness causes you more problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really good question. I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. \n\nI guess... well, they kind of go hand in hand, don't they? If I had to choose, maybe the focus part is a little trickier, just because that's what makes me forget things or lose track of what I'm doing for the house or the kids. The restlessness is there, but I try to just channel that into keeping busy with chores so it's not bothering anyone else. \n\nBut really, I don't know if one is definitively worse than the other. I just... I try to manage both as best I can so they don't get in the way too much. Is it okay if I don't have a clear answer for which one is worse? I hope that's alright.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your symptoms have been present in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in other activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 41 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"The fallout has been that people at home and in my volunteer circles can’t reliably count on me, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I keep letting things fall through despite trying hard.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... yes, I suppose it happens in a few different places. Mostly at home, like I mentioned, but... um, also with my volunteer work. 

I help out with the PTA and a few community things, and I really, really love doing it. But... well, I guess the fallout lately has been that people at home and in my volunteer circles can't always reliably count on me. Which is awful to say out loud, because I really do try so hard to be dependable. 

I've actually had to step back from some of my responsibilities recently because I just... I kept letting things fall through the cracks, despite my best efforts. I didn't want to be a burden or mess up their events, so I thought it was best to just take a step back so I wouldn't let anyone else down. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really am trying to fix it. Does that answer what you were asking about the different settings?

I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're not talking to someone, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... doing things without thinking them through? Oh... um, consistent? I guess... well, yes, I suppose it's always been there, if I really think about it.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... no. No, I've never been officially diagnosed with anything like that. I always just assumed I was a bit scattered or that it was just a personal flaw I needed to try harder to fix, so I never really brought it up to a doctor before. I hope I'm not wasting your time by coming in without any kind of prior paperwork or anything like that. 

And as for my family... um, no, no one has a diagnosis either, as far as I know. I mean, my dad was always kind of on the go, always had to be doing something in the garage or the yard, but nobody ever called it a medical thing. Everyone just said he was a hard worker. 

So, no, nothing official for me or anyone else. Is... is it okay that I'm coming to you without a clear family history of it? I just really want to figure out how to be better and more reliable for my family.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you so much for having me. Um, I hope I'm in the right place for this. \n\nWhat brings me in is... well, lately I just feel like I can’t really settle in my own body anymore? It's probably just a small thing, honestly, but I've been noticing it a lot when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and handle paperwork, or when I need to sit down to figure out shared household plans. It's just really hard to stay put. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay. I just thought it might be good to talk to someone about it, if that's alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, um... restless is exactly it. Like I was just mentioning, it's this feeling that I really can't settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I'm supposed to just sit quietly to handle paperwork, or... or when I need to sit down for those shared household plans. \n\nIt's like I just have this urge to move? I mean, I know everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, and I really don't want to make a huge deal out of it if it's just a normal thing that happens at my age. But yes, restless is a very good word for it. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on the paperwork or plans when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Um, yes... I think so. I feel a little embarrassed admitting it, to be honest, because I really do try my best to pay attention. I promise I'm not just trying to brush things off. \n\nBut, well... But then, after just a few minutes, I'll catch myself just staring at the exact same paragraph. I feel awful when it happens because I don't want people to think I'm ignoring them. Did I... did I answer your question okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're having trouble focusing or sitting still?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, anxious? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I definitely feel bad about it, like I mentioned, because I don't want to let anyone down or seem lazy. But I wouldn't say I feel worried or, you know, panicked about it. \n\nIt's more just... frustrating, I guess? But I try to stay positive! I really don't think it's a big anxiety thing, if that's what you mean. I'm generally a pretty happy person, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not being too confusing.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble sitting still and focusing?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, when did it start? I guess... if I'm really looking back, I think it was already a part of me way back in elementary school. \n\nI feel a little silly admitting this, because I really did try to be a good student, I promise! But... well, I was kind of the little girl with the constantly messy desk. And I was always forgetting my permission slips, things like that. My teachers would sometimes write notes home saying I talked out of turn, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat. \n\nI know all kids are a little squirmy and talkative, so I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it was. I wasn't trying to be disruptive, I just had a lot of energy, I think! But yes, it goes back that far. Is that... is that the kind of timeline you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... that's a tough one to admit. I really, really care about being respectful, and I know how rude it can seem to cut people off. I always try to be a good listener, I promise. \n\nBut... well, sometimes with my husband, or even friends, I'll just... jump into the conversation. Or I'll finish their thoughts for them. It's not because I'm trying to be bossy or anything! It's just... the response just sort of pops out of my mouth before I've even had a chance to tell myself to wait. \n\nI feel terrible every time it happens, because I don't want anyone to think I don't care about what they have to say. I just get so eager to agree or share, you know? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible conversationalist. Is that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers in situations other than with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess I sort of already mentioned it, but... yes, it does happen with other people sometimes, not just my husband. \n\nI really do try so hard to be polite, because I know how it looks when someone just talks over you. But... if I'm in a group, or even just chatting with someone I don't know as well, I might still accidentally jump in or finish their sentence for them. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. \n\nI always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're not talking to someone, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... doing things without thinking them through? I... I don't think so? I really try to be careful and responsible, especially with, you know, daily life stuff. I'm a stay-at-home mom, so I have to keep things running smoothly, and I try my best to be organized. \n\nI mean, I guess everyone has moments where they buy something small at the store without planning to, or maybe decide to bake cookies on a whim, but... no, I wouldn't say I do anything big or risky without thinking it over. I always want to make sure I'm making good choices for my family. \n\nSo, I don't think I really have a problem with that kind of impulsivity. I hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had difficulty keeping a job or maintaining relationships because of your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... well, since I'm a stay-at-home mom, I haven't been in a traditional job for a while, so I don't have to worry about a boss firing me or anything like that. But... oh, this is a little hard to admit, because I really, really pride myself on being helpful and dependable. \n\nI guess... well, the fallout lately has been that my family at home, and the people in my volunteer circles... they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me as much as I'd want them to. \n\nI try so, so hard, I promise I do! I always have the best intentions. But sometimes I just... I keep letting things fall through the cracks. Because of that, I've actually had to step back from some of my volunteer responsibilities recently. I just didn't want to keep accidentally letting people down, you know? \n\nIt feels really awful to say that out loud, because I hate the idea of being seen as flaky or unreliable. I really hope you don't think I'm just lazy or making excuses. I just want to do a good job for everyone and keep the peace. Does... does that make sense for what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your restlessness or difficulty focusing has significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I suppose that really ties into what I was just mentioning a moment ago. It's... well, like I was saying, the main fallout has just been that the people at home, and the other ladies in my volunteer groups, they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me the way I want them to. \n\nAnd it's really not because I don't care! I promise I try so, so hard to stay on top of everything for them. But my mind just gets so scattered, and despite my absolute best efforts, I just keep letting things fall through the cracks. It got to the point where I actually had to step back from some of those volunteer responsibilities, which honestly broke my heart, because I love helping out. I just... I didn't want my scatteredness to keep making things harder for everyone else, you know? \n\nI really try to be a good mom and a good friend. I just struggle to keep all the plates spinning sometimes. Does... does that answer what you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I think I've always been a little bit... you know, busy-minded? But it definitely hasn't always been this hard to manage. \n\nActually... and I really hope this doesn't sound like I'm complaining or making excuses, because my husband is wonderful and I love our life... but it has been noticeably worse since we got married. That was about a year and five months ago. When our routines merged, it just... it became a lot harder to keep everything organized. \n\nI really, really wanted to make the transition seamless for everyone and be a great wife, but combining our daily lives just made things feel a bit more overwhelming for me, and that scattered feeling definitely spiked. \n\nIs it normal for things to get a little harder when you have to adjust to someone else's schedule like that? I just don't want you to think I'm not trying my absolute best to adapt. Does... does that help explain the timeline a little bit?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are causing you a lot of distress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, distress? I mean... I wouldn't say it's like, severe emotional pain or anything like that. I really don't want to sound dramatic. \n\nI guess it's just... well, it's frustrating, you know? Because I really want to be on top of everything for my husband and my kids. When I mess up or forget something important, I do feel pretty bad about it. It makes me feel like I'm letting them down, and that part is hard. But I wouldn't say I'm in deep distress or anything. I just want to figure out how to do better for them. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making too big a deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing or your restlessness causes you more problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really good question. I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. \n\nI guess... well, they kind of go hand in hand, don't they? If I had to choose, maybe the focus part is a little trickier, just because that's what makes me forget things or lose track of what I'm doing for the house or the kids. The restlessness is there, but I try to just channel that into keeping busy with chores so it's not bothering anyone else. \n\nBut really, I don't know if one is definitively worse than the other. I just... I try to manage both as best I can so they don't get in the way too much. Is it okay if I don't have a clear answer for which one is worse? I hope that's alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have been present in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in other activities?\n\nPatient: Well... yes, I suppose it happens in a few different places. Mostly at home, like I mentioned, but... um, also with my volunteer work. \n\nI help out with the PTA and a few community things, and I really, really love doing it. But... well, I guess the fallout lately has been that people at home and in my volunteer circles can't always reliably count on me. Which is awful to say out loud, because I really do try so hard to be dependable. \n\nI've actually had to step back from some of my responsibilities recently because I just... I kept letting things fall through the cracks, despite my best efforts. I didn't want to be a burden or mess up their events, so I thought it was best to just take a step back so I wouldn't let anyone else down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really am trying to fix it. Does that answer what you were asking about the different settings?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing at a relatively consistent level throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, consistent? I guess... well, yes, I suppose it's always been there, if I really think about it. \n\nI mean, I don't want to make it sound like I've been a mess my whole life, but... looking back, even in elementary school, I was always kind of that girl. You know, the one with the constantly messy desk, or the one who always forgot her permission slips at home. My mom used to get so frustrated. And I remember getting notes sent home from teachers saying I talked out of turn too much, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat when I was supposed to. \n\nI always just thought I was a little too energetic or maybe a bit scatterbrained, and I really tried to be a good student. I hope it doesn't sound like I was a bad kid or anything—I was always trying my best to be good. But yes, I guess those things have kind of been with me for as long as I can remember. Is that the kind of consistency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, I've never been officially diagnosed with anything like that. I always just assumed I was a bit scattered or that it was just a personal flaw I needed to try harder to fix, so I never really brought it up to a doctor before. I hope I'm not wasting your time by coming in without any kind of prior paperwork or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for my family... um, no, no one has a diagnosis either, as far as I know. I mean, my dad was always kind of on the go, always had to be doing something in the garage or the yard, but nobody ever called it a medical thing. Everyone just said he was a hard worker. \n\nSo, no, nothing official for me or anyone else. Is... is it okay that I'm coming to you without a clear family history of it? I just really want to figure out how to be better and more reliable for my family.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well... I mean, I try really hard to keep things organized. I have planners and color-coded folders, and I really make an effort to keep everything running smoothly for the family. 

I guess... sometimes things do get a little bit overwhelming? Like, if there are a lot of steps to something, say, planning a big dinner or organizing a school event... I just get so eager to agree or share, you know? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible conversationalist. Is that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers in situations other than with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess I sort of already mentioned it, but... yes, it does happen with other people sometimes, not just my husband. \n\nI really do try so hard to be polite, because I know how it looks when someone just talks over you. But... if I'm in a group, or even just chatting with someone I don't know as well, I might still accidentally jump in or finish their sentence for them. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. \n\nI always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're not talking to someone, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... doing things without thinking them through? I... I don't think so? I really try to be careful and responsible, especially with, you know, daily life stuff. I'm a stay-at-home mom, so I have to keep things running smoothly, and I try my best to be organized. \n\nI mean, I guess everyone has moments where they buy something small at the store without planning to, or maybe decide to bake cookies on a whim, but... no, I wouldn't say I do anything big or risky without thinking it over. I always want to make sure I'm making good choices for my family. \n\nSo, I don't think I really have a problem with that kind of impulsivity. I hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had difficulty keeping a job or maintaining relationships because of your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... well, since I'm a stay-at-home mom, I haven't been in a traditional job for a while, so I don't have to worry about a boss firing me or anything like that. But... oh, this is a little hard to admit, because I really, really pride myself on being helpful and dependable. \n\nI guess... well, the fallout lately has been that my family at home, and the people in my volunteer circles... they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me as much as I'd want them to. \n\nI try so, so hard, I promise I do! I always have the best intentions. But sometimes I just... I keep letting things fall through the cracks. Because of that, I've actually had to step back from some of my volunteer responsibilities recently. I just didn't want to keep accidentally letting people down, you know? \n\nIt feels really awful to say that out loud, because I hate the idea of being seen as flaky or unreliable. I really hope you don't think I'm just lazy or making excuses. I just want to do a good job for everyone and keep the peace. Does... does that make sense for what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your restlessness or difficulty focusing has significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I suppose that really ties into what I was just mentioning a moment ago. It's... well, like I was saying, the main fallout has just been that the people at home, and the other ladies in my volunteer groups, they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me the way I want them to. \n\nAnd it's really not because I don't care! I promise I try so, so hard to stay on top of everything for them. But my mind just gets so scattered, and despite my absolute best efforts, I just keep letting things fall through the cracks. It got to the point where I actually had to step back from some of those volunteer responsibilities, which honestly broke my heart, because I love helping out. I just... I didn't want my scatteredness to keep making things harder for everyone else, you know? \n\nI really try to be a good mom and a good friend. I just struggle to keep all the plates spinning sometimes. Does... does that answer what you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I think I've always been a little bit... you know, busy-minded? But it definitely hasn't always been this hard to manage. \n\nActually... and I really hope this doesn't sound like I'm complaining or making excuses, because my husband is wonderful and I love our life... but it has been noticeably worse since we got married. That was about a year and five months ago. When our routines merged, it just... it became a lot harder to keep everything organized. \n\nI really, really wanted to make the transition seamless for everyone and be a great wife, but combining our daily lives just made things feel a bit more overwhelming for me, and that scattered feeling definitely spiked. \n\nIs it normal for things to get a little harder when you have to adjust to someone else's schedule like that? Oh... um... well, I really want to say no, because I love my family so much and I try so, so hard to be there for them. I really do.

I feel so guilty about it, like I'm failing them. I really hope you don't think I'm a bad mom or a bad wife because of this. I just want to fix it so I can be the person they need me to be. Is it... is it normal to feel this way?

I mean, I know everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, and I really don't want to make a huge deal out of it if it's just a normal thing that happens at my age. But yes, restless is a very good word for it. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on the paperwork or plans when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Um, yes... I think so. I feel a little embarrassed admitting it, to be honest, because I really do try my best to pay attention. I promise I'm not just trying to brush things off. \n\nBut, well... I can sit down with a planning email, or even just a chapter of a book, and I always start out completely fine. I'm ready to focus. But then, after just a few minutes, I'll catch myself just staring at the exact same paragraph. And I'll realize that absolutely none of it actually went in. \n\nI don't want to sound like I'm making excuses for myself, and I'm sure it's probably just normal distraction, right? I feel awful when it happens because I don't want people to think I'm ignoring them. Did I... did I answer your question okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're having trouble focusing or sitting still?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, anxious? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I definitely feel bad about it, like I mentioned, because I don't want to let anyone down or seem lazy. But I wouldn't say I feel worried or, you know, panicked about it. \n\nIt's more just... frustrating, I guess? But I try to stay positive! I really don't think it's a big anxiety thing, if that's what you mean. I'm generally a pretty happy person, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not being too confusing.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble sitting still and focusing?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, when did it start? I guess... if I'm really looking back, I think it was already a part of me way back in elementary school. \n\nI feel a little silly admitting this, because I really did try to be a good student, I promise! But... well, I was kind of the little girl with the constantly messy desk. And I was always forgetting my permission slips, things like that. My teachers would sometimes write notes home saying I talked out of turn, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat. \n\nI know all kids are a little squirmy and talkative, so I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it was. I wasn't trying to be disruptive, I just had a lot of energy, I think! But yes, it goes back that far. Is that... is that the kind of timeline you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... that's a tough one to admit. I really, really care about being respectful, and I know how rude it can seem to cut people off. I always try to be a good listener, I promise. \n\nBut... well, sometimes with my husband, or even friends, I'll just... jump into the conversation. Or I'll finish their thoughts for them. It's not because I'm trying to be bossy or anything! It's just... the response just sort of pops out of my mouth before I've even had a chance to tell myself to wait. \n\nI feel terrible every time it happens, because I don't want anyone to think I don't care about what they have to say. I just get so eager to agree or share, you know? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible conversationalist. Is that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers in situations other than with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess I sort of already mentioned it, but... yes, it does happen with other people sometimes, not just my husband. \n\nI really do try so hard to be polite, because I know how it looks when someone just talks over you. But... if I'm in a group, or even just chatting with someone I don't know as well, I might still accidentally jump in or finish their sentence for them. It's just... like I said, the words just tumble out before my brain can tell my mouth to stop and wait its turn. \n\nI always apologize right away, I promise! I never want anyone to feel like I'm taking over the conversation or being rude. I just get a little too enthusiastic, maybe? I hope that makes sense... I'm really not trying to be a difficult person to talk to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're not talking to someone, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... doing things without thinking them through? I... I don't think so? I really try to be careful and responsible, especially with, you know, daily life stuff. I'm a stay-at-home mom, so I have to keep things running smoothly, and I try my best to be organized. \n\nI mean, I guess everyone has moments where they buy something small at the store without planning to, or maybe decide to bake cookies on a whim, but... no, I wouldn't say I do anything big or risky without thinking it over. I always want to make sure I'm making good choices for my family. \n\nSo, I don't think I really have a problem with that kind of impulsivity. I hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had difficulty keeping a job or maintaining relationships because of your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... well, since I'm a stay-at-home mom, I haven't been in a traditional job for a while, so I don't have to worry about a boss firing me or anything like that. But... oh, this is a little hard to admit, because I really, really pride myself on being helpful and dependable. \n\nI guess... well, the fallout lately has been that my family at home, and the people in my volunteer circles... they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me as much as I'd want them to. \n\nI try so, so hard, I promise I do! I always have the best intentions. But sometimes I just... I keep letting things fall through the cracks. Because of that, I've actually had to step back from some of my volunteer responsibilities recently. I just didn't want to keep accidentally letting people down, you know? \n\nIt feels really awful to say that out loud, because I hate the idea of being seen as flaky or unreliable. I really hope you don't think I'm just lazy or making excuses. I just want to do a good job for everyone and keep the peace. Does... does that make sense for what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your restlessness or difficulty focusing has significantly interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I suppose that really ties into what I was just mentioning a moment ago. It's... well, like I was saying, the main fallout has just been that the people at home, and the other ladies in my volunteer groups, they maybe feel like they can't reliably count on me the way I want them to. \n\nAnd it's really not because I don't care! I promise I try so, so hard to stay on top of everything for them. But my mind just gets so scattered, and despite my absolute best efforts, I just keep letting things fall through the cracks. It got to the point where I actually had to step back from some of those volunteer responsibilities, which honestly broke my heart, because I love helping out. I just... I didn't want my scatteredness to keep making things harder for everyone else, you know? \n\nI really try to be a good mom and a good friend. I just struggle to keep all the plates spinning sometimes. Does... does that answer what you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I think I've always been a little bit... you know, busy-minded? But it definitely hasn't always been this hard to manage. \n\nActually... and I really hope this doesn't sound like I'm complaining or making excuses, because my husband is wonderful and I love our life... but it has been noticeably worse since we got married. That was about a year and five months ago. When our routines merged, it just... it became a lot harder to keep everything organized. \n\nI really, really wanted to make the transition seamless for everyone and be a great wife, but combining our daily lives just made things feel a bit more overwhelming for me, and that scattered feeling definitely spiked. \n\nIs it normal for things to get a little harder when you have to adjust to someone else's schedule like that? I just don't want you to think I'm not trying my absolute best to adapt. Does... does that help explain the timeline a little bit?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are causing you a lot of distress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, distress? I mean... I wouldn't say it's like, severe emotional pain or anything like that. I really don't want to sound dramatic. \n\nI guess it's just... well, it's frustrating, you know? Because I really want to be on top of everything for my husband and my kids. When I mess up or forget something important, I do feel pretty bad about it. It makes me feel like I'm letting them down, and that part is hard. But I wouldn't say I'm in deep distress or anything. I just want to figure out how to do better for them. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making too big a deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing or your restlessness causes you more problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really good question. I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. \n\nI guess... well, they kind of go hand in hand, don't they? If I had to choose, maybe the focus part is a little trickier, just because that's what makes me forget things or lose track of what I'm doing for the house or the kids. The restlessness is there, but I try to just channel that into keeping busy with chores so it's not bothering anyone else. \n\nBut really, I don't know if one is definitively worse than the other. I just... I try to manage both as best I can so they don't get in the way too much. Is it okay if I don't have a clear answer for which one is worse? I hope that's alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have been present in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in other activities?\n\nPatient: Well... yes, I suppose it happens in a few different places. Mostly at home, like I mentioned, but... um, also with my volunteer work. \n\nI help out with the PTA and a few community things, and I really, really love doing it. But... well, I guess the fallout lately has been that people at home and in my volunteer circles can't always reliably count on me. Which is awful to say out loud, because I really do try so hard to be dependable. \n\nI've actually had to step back from some of my responsibilities recently because I just... I kept letting things fall through the cracks, despite my best efforts. I didn't want to be a burden or mess up their events, so I thought it was best to just take a step back so I wouldn't let anyone else down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really am trying to fix it. Does that answer what you were asking about the different settings?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing at a relatively consistent level throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, consistent? I guess... well, yes, I suppose it's always been there, if I really think about it. \n\nI mean, I don't want to make it sound like I've been a mess my whole life, but... looking back, even in elementary school, I was always kind of that girl. You know, the one with the constantly messy desk, or the one who always forgot her permission slips at home. My mom used to get so frustrated. And I remember getting notes sent home from teachers saying I talked out of turn too much, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat when I was supposed to. \n\nI always just thought I was a little too energetic or maybe a bit scatterbrained, and I really tried to be a good student. I hope it doesn't sound like I was a bad kid or anything—I was always trying my best to be good. But yes, I guess those things have kind of been with me for as long as I can remember. Is that the kind of consistency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, I've never been officially diagnosed with anything like that. I always just assumed I was a bit scattered or that it was just a personal flaw I needed to try harder to fix, so I never really brought it up to a doctor before. I hope I'm not wasting your time by coming in without any kind of prior paperwork or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for my family... um, no, no one has a diagnosis either, as far as I know. I mean, my dad was always kind of on the go, always had to be doing something in the garage or the yard, but nobody ever called it a medical thing. Everyone just said he was a hard worker. \n\nSo, no, nothing official for me or anyone else. Is... is it okay that I'm coming to you without a clear family history of it? I just really want to figure out how to be better and more reliable for my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well... oh, I hate to say yes to that, because I really do pride myself on trying to do a good job. But... yes, I suppose I do. \n\nI really try to be careful, I promise I do. But, for example, I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet for one of my committees, or I'll sit down to do our household budget. I just somehow don't see the errors until it's too late. I hope you don't think I'm just being lazy with my responsibilities. I mean, I try really hard to keep things organized. I didn't want my scatteredness to keep making things harder for everyone else, you know? \n\nI really try to be a good mom and a good friend. I just struggle to keep all the plates spinning sometimes. Does... does that answer what you were looking for? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I think I've always been a little bit... you know, busy-minded? But it definitely hasn't always been this hard to manage. \n\nActually... and I really hope this doesn't sound like I'm complaining or making excuses, because my husband is wonderful and I love our life... but it has been noticeably worse since we got married. That was about a year and five months ago. When our routines merged, it just... it became a lot harder to keep everything organized. \n\nI really, really wanted to make the transition seamless for everyone and be a great wife, but combining our daily lives just made things feel a bit more overwhelming for me, and that scattered feeling definitely spiked. \n\nIs it normal for things to get a little harder when you have to adjust to someone else's schedule like that? I just don't want you to think I'm not trying my absolute best to adapt. Does... does that help explain the timeline a little bit?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are causing you a lot of distress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, distress? I mean... I wouldn't say it's like, severe emotional pain or anything like that. I really don't want to sound dramatic. \n\nI guess it's just... well, it's frustrating, you know? Because I really want to be on top of everything for my husband and my kids. When I mess up or forget something important, I do feel pretty bad about it. It makes me feel like I'm letting them down, and that part is hard. But I wouldn't say I'm in deep distress or anything. I just want to figure out how to do better for them. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making too big a deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing or your restlessness causes you more problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really good question. I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. \n\nI guess... well, they kind of go hand in hand, don't they? If I had to choose, maybe the focus part is a little trickier, just because that's what makes me forget things or lose track of what I'm doing for the house or the kids. The restlessness is there, but I try to just channel that into keeping busy with chores so it's not bothering anyone else. \n\nBut really, I don't know if one is definitively worse than the other. I just... I try to manage both as best I can so they don't get in the way too much. Is it okay if I don't have a clear answer for which one is worse? I hope that's alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have been present in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in other activities?\n\nPatient: Well... yes, I suppose it happens in a few different places. I kept letting things fall through the cracks, despite my best efforts. I didn't want to be a burden or mess up their events, so I thought it was best to just take a step back so I wouldn't let anyone else down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really am trying to fix it. Does that answer what you were asking about the different settings?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing at a relatively consistent level throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Oh... um, consistent? I guess... well, yes, I suppose it's always been there, if I really think about it. \n\nI mean, I don't want to make it sound like I've been a mess my whole life, but... looking back, even in elementary school, I was always kind of that girl. You know, the one with the constantly messy desk, or the one who always forgot her permission slips at home. My mom used to get so frustrated. And I remember getting notes sent home from teachers saying I talked out of turn too much, or that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat when I was supposed to. \n\nI always just thought I was a little too energetic or maybe a bit scatterbrained, and I really tried to be a good student. I hope it doesn't sound like I was a bad kid or anything—I was always trying my best to be good. But yes, I guess those things have kind of been with me for as long as I can remember. Is that the kind of consistency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, I've never been officially diagnosed with anything like that. I always just assumed I was a bit scattered or that it was just a personal flaw I needed to try harder to fix, so I never really brought it up to a doctor before. I hope I'm not wasting your time by coming in without any kind of prior paperwork or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for my family... um, no, no one has a diagnosis either, as far as I know. Everyone just said he was a hard worker. \n\nSo, no, nothing official for me or anyone else. Is... is it okay that I'm coming to you without a clear family history of it? I just really want to figure out how to be better and more reliable for my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well... oh, I hate to say yes to that, because I really do pride myself on trying to do a good job. I just somehow don't see the errors until it's too late. I hope you don't think I'm just being lazy with my responsibilities. Is... is that the kind of mistake you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Well... I have planners and color-coded folders, and I really make an effort to keep everything running smoothly for the family. \n\nI guess... sometimes things do get a little bit overwhelming? Like, if there are a lot of steps to something, say, planning a big dinner or organizing a school event... I might start out really strong, but then the details kind of get tangled up. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining this right.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing significantly impacted your ability to maintain your role as a stay-at-home mom or your relationships with your family?\n\nPatient: Oh... um... well, I really want to say no, because I love my family so much and I try so, so hard to be there for them.
